<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2025 Jun 24; Revised 2026 Jan 25; Accepted 2026 Jan 28; Collection date 2026 Feb.</p></section></div></div>

</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="ab0005"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p><em>Poria cocos</em> is a commonly utilized traditional Chinese dietary ingredient for tea, soup, porridge, baking, and herbal formulas. To compare the effects of different processing techniques on triterpenoids, six representative <em>Poria cocos</em> decoction pieces were selected through market analysis, and a novel analysis method was established based on the SIRIUS-MCnebula in combination with UPLC-QTOF-MS/MS. 211 triterpenoids were summarized by classyfireR, and 56 were identified. The results showed significant differences in the frequency of occurrence of different types of parent nuclei and side chains, among which Lanoster-8-ene triterpenoid and m-type are the highest. The shade drying group contained richer triterpenoid components and higher content compared to the non-heat-treated groups (sun-dried, fresh-cut, sweat-treated) and the heat-treated groups (steam-treated, sweat and steam-treated). This research enhances how processing techniques influence the triterpenoid components of <em>Poria cocos</em> and provides scientific evidence for optimizing <em>Poria cocos</em> processing techniques, emphasizing the potential of AI technology in food research.</p>
<section id="ks0005" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> <em>Poria cocos</em>, Processing techniques, SIRIUS-MCnebula, Chemical compounds identification, UPLC-QTOF-MS/MS</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="ab0010"><h2>Graphical abstract</h2>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0065" title="Unlabelled Image"><p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_ga1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/43b3d13d48f2/ga1.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0065" height="443" width="786" alt="Unlabelled Image"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0065/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure></section><section class="abstract" id="ab0015"><h2>Highlights</h2>
<ul id="l0005" class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li id="li0005">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0005">SIRIUS‑MCnebula was developed to compare the triterpenoids in crude and processed <em>Poria cocos</em>.</p>
</li>
<li id="li0010">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0010">211 triterpenoids in <em>Poria cocos</em> were summarized by classyfireR.</p>
</li>
<li id="li0015">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0015">A total of 56 differentially expressed triterpenoids were identified.</p>
</li>
<li id="li0020">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0020">The YG <em>Poria cocos</em> demonstrated excellent performance in terms of relative quantification.</p>
</li>
</ul></section><section id="s0005"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p id="p0030"><em>Poria cocos</em> (Schw.) Wolf, synonyms <em>Wolfiporia</em> cocos (<a href="#bb0170" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0170">Liu et al., 2024</a>), commonly known as “Fuling” in Chinese, is a widely distributed medicinal fungus (<a href="#bb0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0210">Ríos, 2011</a>) predominantly found near weathered or decaying pine tree roots. Rich in bioactive compounds and rooted in centuries-old medicinal tradition, <em>Poria cocos</em> exhibits diuretic and dampness-eliminating properties. It is extensively used not only in traditional Chinese medicine but also in health supplements and functional food products (<a href="#bb0225" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0225">Sun et al., 2017</a>). Its appeal extends well beyond China, capturing the growing global health product market (<a href="#bb0155" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0155">Li, Zhang, Li, Liu and Wang, 2016</a>). With increasing demand, it is seamlessly incorporated into a variety of offerings, including innovative culinary creations such as soups and savory, as well as health-conscious snacks, desserts, and even everyday dairy products.</p>
<p id="p0035">The processing techniques applied to <em>Poria cocos</em> are varied and have a significant impact on the morphology, taste, chemical composition and bioactivity of the final product. Based on both market analysis and literature review, six primary processing techniques are commonly employed for <em>Poria cocos</em>: sun-drying (SG), fresh-cutting (XQ), sweat treatment (FH), steam treatment (ZH), combined sweat and steam treatment (FHZH), and shade drying (YG). These distinct processing approaches markedly influence the chemical constituents and pharmacological properties of <em>Poria cocos</em> (<a href="#bb0085" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0085">Fu et al., 2018</a>). However, a comprehensive and systematic investigation into the alterations in the material basis resulting from these different processing methods remains lacking.</p>
<p id="p0040">This study presents the development of a novel mass spectrometric data analysis approach, termed “SIRIUS-Mcnebula,” designed to identify and compare alterations in the chemical constituents of across six distinct processing techniques <em>Poria cocos</em>. And SIRIUS-MCnebula and the integrated approach of GNPS combined with UPLC-QTOF-MS/MS molecular network analysis were compared. The findings elucidate the chemical compositional differences and reasons among the SG, XQ, FH, ZH, FHZH, and YG group, thereby establishing a chemical basis for <em>Poria cocos</em> decoction pieces with the diverse processing techniques and supporting their practical applications. This not only enriches the understanding of the market rationality of the processing technology of <em>Poria cocos</em> during different processing but also emphasizes the importance of processing methods in making full use of their differences (<a href="#bib351" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib351">Zhang et al., 2019</a>).</p></section><section id="s0010"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials and methods</h2>
<section id="s0015"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Reagents and materials</h3>
<p id="p0045">Acetonitrile and methanol of mass spectrometry grade were procured from Sigma-Aldrich (St.Louis, MO, USA). Double-distilled water (ddH<sub>2</sub>O) was employed in all experimental procedures. Analytical-grade reagents, except for those specifically mentioned, were obtained from Sinopharm Chemical Reagent Co., Ltd., located in Shanghai, China.</p>
<p id="p0050">The reference standards of Poria acid A (Lot number: M UST-19032203, purity ≥98.98%), Poria acid B (Lot number: MUST-19031001, purity ≥98%), Dehydropachymic acid (Lot number: MUST-19030620, purity ≥98%), and Dehydrotrametenolic acid (Lot number: MUST-19032403, purity ≥98%) were purchased from Shanghai YuanYe Biotechnology Co., Ltd.</p></section><section id="s0020"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Instrumentation</h3>
<p id="p0055">LC-MS/MS analysis was performed on a UPLC system (Acquity I-Class, Waters Corporation, Milford, MA, USA) coupled to a Xevo G2-XS QToF mass spectrometer (Waters Corporation, Manchester, UK), utilizing a Waters HSS T3 column (1.8 μm, 100 mm × 2.1 mm). Additionally, the auxiliary equipment of the UPLC system included a KQ-500 dB ultrasonic cleaning bath (Kunshan Ultrasonic Instrument Co., Ltd.), an SQP-SECURA225D-1CN microbalance and a BSA124S analytical balance (both from Sartorius Scientific Instruments (Beijing) Co., Ltd.), and a UNIQUE-R20 ultrapure water system (Risj Science Instrument Co., Ltd.).</p></section><section id="s0025"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Preparation of different processed <em>Poria cocos</em>
</h3>
<p id="p0060">Fresh <em>Poria cocos</em> samples were purchased from the Jinzhai <em>Poria cocos</em> Cultivation Base, Anhui Province, China. And the decoction pieces were prepared by the Processing Center of Zhejiang Chinese Medical University. The mature fruiting body was authenticated by Researcher Cao Gang from the School of Pharmaceutical Sciences, Zhejiang Chinese Medical University, as belonging to the fungus <em>Poria cocos</em> (Schw.) Wolf. Based on our research (<a href="#bb0075" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0075">Dongyang et al., 2025</a>), six representative <em>Poria cocos</em> decoction pieces have been prepared, and the processing techniques are detailed in <a href="#t0005" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="t0005"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Grouping and processing parameters of <em>Poria cocos.</em></p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead><tr>
<th colspan="1" rowspan="1">Group</th>
<th colspan="1" rowspan="1">Processing techniques</th>
</tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Fresh-cut (XQ)</td>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Peel the fresh <em>Poria cocos</em>, slice into 4 mm thick pieces, and dry at a low temperature of 50 °C</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Sun-dried (SG)</td>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Peel the fresh <em>Poria cocos</em>, slice into 4 mm thick pieces, and dry naturally under sunlight.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Sweat-treated (FH)</td>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Fresh <em>Poria cocos</em> were wrapped in polyethylene film and subsequently stacked, covered with nylon plastic sheeting. The samples underwent a sweating process 3 times over a total duration of 9 days. Following this, peel the <em>Poria cocos</em>, slice into 4 mm thick pieces, and dry at a low temperature of 50 °C.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Shade-dried (YG)</td>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Peel the fresh <em>Poria cocos</em>, slice into 4 mm thick pieces, and ferment for 72 h at a temperature of 23 °C and a relative humidity of 90%. Following fermentation, the samples should be dried naturally in a well-ventilated area, protected from direct sunlight.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Steam-treated (ZH)</td>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Fresh <em>Poria cocos</em> should be thoroughly washed and then steamed in a covered environment. Once the water temperature attains 100 °C and the temperature within the steamer is uniformly stabilized, initiate the timing. Continue steaming for a duration of 40 min. Subsequently, peel the <em>Poria cocos</em>, slice it into 4 mm thick pieces, and dry at a low temperature of 50 °C.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Sweat and steam-treated (FHZH)</td>
<td colspan="1" rowspan="1">Fresh <em>Poria cocos</em> were wrapped in polyethylene film and subsequently stacked, covered with nylon plastic sheeting. The samples underwent a sweating process 3 times over a total duration of 9 days. Subsequently, the <em>Poria cocos</em> was steamed in a steamer for 40 min at 100 °C. Following steaming, peel the <em>Poria cocos</em>, slice into 4 mm thick pieces, and dry at a low temperature of 50 °C.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/t0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="s0030"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Preparation of test solution</h3>
<p id="p0065">Processed <em>Poria cocos</em> was ground to a fine powder (80-mesh, No. 5 sieve). Approximately 10 g of each powder (accurately weighed) was transferred into a stoppered conical flask, to which 20 mL of 75% ethanol was added. The total flask mass was recorded, then the mixture was sonicated (250 W, 40 kHz) for 30 min. After cooling, the flask was re-weighed, and any loss in mass was made up with 75% ethanol. The extract was centrifuged at 12,000 rpm (12 cm radius) for 10 min at 4 °C. The supernatant was filtered through a 0.22 μm PTFE membrane and stored at −80 °C until UPLC-MS/MS analysis.</p>
<p id="p0070">After combining aliquots of each of the six treatment groups, 20 μL of each group extract (total, 120 μL) was placed in a microcentrifuge tube. The pooled quality control (QC) samples were vortex-mixed for 3 min, centrifuged at 15,000 rpm for 10 min at 4 °C, and the resulting supernatant was aliquoted into labeled Eppendorf tubes. QC aliquots were stored at −80 °C alongside study samples. To monitor analytical stability, one QC injection was inserted after every five study-sample injections.</p></section><section id="s0035"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. UPLC-ESI-MS/MS analysis</h3>
<p id="p0075">Solvent A (formic acid/water (0.1:99, <em>v</em>/v)) and solvent B (formic acid/acetonitrile (0.1:99, v/v)) were employed as the mobile phase. The elution gradient used for separation was as follows: 50% solvent B at 0 min; 55% solvent B at 4 min; 58% solvent B at 9 min; 68% solvent B at 10 min; 80% solvent B at 15 min; 85% solvent B at 17 min; returning to 50% solvent B at 18 min, which was maintained until 19 min. The flow rate was set at 0.3 mL/min, and the column temperature was controlled at 35 °C.</p>
<p id="p0080">The mass spectrometry operation parameters were set as follows: Ionization mode, EIC; Collision Energy, 6 eV; Cone Voltage, 45 V; MSMS Data Format, Centroid. The full scan mass range for MS was set from <em>m/z</em> 50 to 1200 Da, while the product ion scan range was from <em>m/z</em> 50 to 800 Da. The column temperature was maintained at 35 °C, and the PDA detection wavelength was set at 254 nm. Data was collected in both positive and negative ionization modes using QTOF-MS/MS.</p>
<p id="p0085">Mass spectrometric analysis was conducted using a QTOF instrument equipped with a Zspray™ ESI source (Waters Corporation, Manchester, UK) operating in both positive and negative ionization modes. The ESI source operated at 120 °C, with a desolvation temperature of 360 °C, a spray voltage of 3.5 kV, a cone gas flow of 50 L/h, and a desolvation gas flow of 800 L/h.</p>
<p id="p0090">For the fast data-dependent acquisition (DDA) setting, survey scans were collected over a mass range of 50–800 Da with a scan time of 0.1 s. Data were acquired in centroid format, and the maximum allowable mass error was set at ±10 ppm. A dual dynamic collision energy function (6–15 eV for low <em>m/z</em> 50 and 60–90 eV for high <em>m/z</em> 800) was applied for measurement scans. Leucine-enkephalin solution (10 ng/L) was infused at a constant flow rate of 10 μL/min for LockSpray calibration. The method was configured to analyze the top five most intense ions from the survey scans, with dynamic exclusion enabled for 15 s.</p>
<p id="p0095">A mixed standard solution was prepared by dissolving each reference standard compound to 100 μg/mL in 75% ethanol and then combining equal volumes to yield a final 25 μg/mL concentration for each analyte. This same mixed solution was used to assess intra-day precision: six independent aliquots were injected under UPLC-MS/MS conditions, and the relative standard deviations (RSDs) of peak areas of four standards calculated were &lt; 2%.</p>
<p id="p0100">Data analysis was carried out using MassLynx V4.1 software (Waters Corporation, Milford, USA).</p></section><section id="s0040"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Chemical compounds identification of characteristic peaks</h3>
<p id="p0105">Prepare an ethanol extract of <em>Poria cocos</em> and analyze it using UPLC-QTOF. After using MSConvert to convert the UPLC-MS/MS raw data of <em>Poria cocos</em> samples from 6 processing techniques into “.mzML” format data, MZmine-2.53 (<a href="#bb0105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0105">Korf et al., 2020</a>) was used to extract characteristic peaks from the negative spectrum. The peak areas, retention time, and secondary negative ionization fragment information of 1622 characteristic peaks were exported as “.csv” and “.mgf” format data. The specific batch processing parameter configuration was shown in <strong>Supplementary Materials S1</strong>.</p>
<p id="p0110">The Global Natural Product Social (GNPS) platform (<a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu</a>) was used to analyze the MS/MS “.mzML” format data. MS/MS spectra were subjected to window filtering based on the top five most intense ion peaks within a ± 50 Da window across the entire spectrum. A network was then created, where edges between nodes were filtered by a cosine value above 0.70 and at least four matching peaks. Nodes that appeared in the network were annotated based on the experimental MS/MS fragmentation. Molecular networks were visualized and laid out using Cytoscape 3.10.2 (<a href="#bb0220" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0220">Shannon et al., 2003</a>).</p>
<p id="p0115">Through PubChem searching, there are 480 natural products classified into <em>Wolfiporia</em> cocos (<a href="https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/taxonomy/81056" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/taxonomy/81056</a>). The PubChem PUG REST application program interface (API) was used to export the InChIKey, SMILES, accurate mass, chemical formula and other information of the substance according to the CID (e.g. <a href="https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/rest/pug/compound/cid/5471851" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/rest/pug/compound/cid/5471851</a>,547,185/property/Title,MolecularFormula,MolecularWeight,ExactMass,InChIKey,SMILES/CSV). According to the chemical structure InChIKey information of the substance, the classyfireR (an R package for chemical classification) package was used to call the ClassyFire REST API to obtain the classification information of the substance.</p>
<p id="p0120">Characteristic peaks “.mgf” format data export from MZmine-2.53 was used for compounds annotations by SIRIUS 5.8.6. Import <em>Poria cocos</em> triterpenoid compounds SMILES into SIRIUS to build a custom database. Set running parameters for the sample to only contain C, H, and O elements, select the custom database, MS2 mass accuracy of 10 ppm, Possible ionizations from [M-H]<sup>−</sup>, and Predict Fingerprints with fallback aducts set to [M-H]<sup>−</sup> and [M-H<sub>2</sub>O-H]<sup>−</sup>.</p>
<p id="p0125">MCnebula (<a href="https://mcnebula.org/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://mcnebula.org/</a>) utilizes the state-of-the-art computer prediction technology, SIRIUS workflow, for compound formula prediction, structure retrieve and classification prediction. With MCnebula, we can switch from untargeted to targeted analysis, focusing precisely on the compound or chemical class of interest to the researcher. The annotation results of the 2.10 SIRIUS mass spectrometry analysis were visualized using the MCnebula2 R package developed by our team, which has functions such as compound classification of characteristic peaks. The parameter settings for the cross_filter_dardust function in MCnebula2 are min_numbers = 60 and max_ratio = 0.55. The code can be found in <strong>Supplementary Materials S2</strong>.</p></section><section id="s0045"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7. Molecular identification and phylogenetic analysis of fungal mycelium</h3>
<p id="p0130">A small amount of YG group mycelium of <em>Poria cocos</em> was mounted on a glass slide with a drop of chloral hydrate solution (10%, <em>w</em>/<em>v</em>), covered with a coverslip, and observed under a Nikon ECLIPSE E200 optical microscope to examine morphological characteristics.</p>
<p id="p0135">Genomic DNA was extracted from the mycelium using the Rapid Fungi Genomic DNA Isolation Kit (B518229, Sangon Biotech, Shanghai) following the manufacturer's protocol. Approximately 50 mg of fresh mycelium was ground into powder in liquid nitrogen and transferred to a 1.5 mL tube. Then, 400 μL of Digestion Buffer and 4 μL of β-mercaptoethanol were added, and the mixture was vortexed and incubated at 65 °C for 1 h to ensure complete cell lysis. After incubation, 20 μL of RNase A (10 mg/mL) was added and kept at room temperature for 5 min. Subsequently, 200 μL of Buffer PF was added and mixed thoroughly, followed by incubation at −20 °C for 5 min. The mixture was centrifuged at 10,000 rpm for 5 min at room temperature, and 550 μL of the supernatant was transferred to a new tube. An equal volume of isopropanol was added, mixed gently by inverting the tube several times, and allowed to stand at room temperature for 2–3 min. After centrifugation at 10,000 rpm for 5 min, the supernatant was discarded. The DNA pellet was washed twice with 1 mL of 75% ethanol, each time followed by centrifugation at 10,000 rpm for 2 min. The pellet was air-dried at room temperature for 10 min to evaporate residual ethanol and finally dissolved in 50 μL of TE buffer (10 mM Tris-HCl, 1 mM EDTA, pH 8.0). The extracted DNA was stored at −20 °C for subsequent use.</p>
<p id="p0140">The internal transcribed spacer (ITS) regions were amplified by PCR using the universal fungal primers ITS1 (5′-TCCGTAGGTGAACCTGCGG-3′) and ITS4 (5′-TCCTCCGCTTATTGATATGC-3′). The PCR reaction mixture (50 μL) contained 1.0 μL genomic DNA (20 ng/μL), 5.0 μL 10× Buffer (containing 2.5 mM Mg<sup>2+</sup>), 0.25 μL exTaq DNA polymerase, 2.0 μL dNTP mixture (10 mM each), 1.0 μL of each primer (10 μM), and 39.75 μL sterile ddH₂O. The thermal cycling conditions were as follows: initial denaturation at 96 °C for 2 min 30 s; followed by 30 cycles of denaturation at 96 °C for 30 s, annealing at 55 °C for 30 s, and extension at 72 °C for 1 min 30 s; with a final extension at 72 °C for 5 min. The amplified products were purified using a silica-based magnetic bead kit and sequenced bidirectionally on an Applied Biosystems 3730xl DNA Analyzer.</p>
<p id="p0145">The obtained sequences were subjected to a Basic Local Alignment Search Tool (BLAST) analysis against the NCBI GenBank nucleotide database (<a href="https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/</a>) for preliminary identification (<a href="#bb0110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0110">Kumar &amp; Shukla, 2005</a>). A phylogenetic tree was constructed using the Neighbor-Joining method based on the Kimura 2-parameter model in MEGA 12.1 software. The confidence of branching patterns was evaluated by bootstrap analysis with 1000 replicates.</p></section><section id="s0050"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.8. Statistical analysis</h3>
<p id="p0150">Principal Component Analysis (PCA) was performed by SIMCA 14.1 to visualize any inherent clustering and identify outliers using Hotelling's T<sup>2</sup> ellipse for strong outliers at the 95% confidence level. The MetaboAnalyst platform (<a href="https://metaboanalyst.ca/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://metaboanalyst.ca/</a>)was used for hierarchical clustering analysis heatmap. For normally distributed measurement data, one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) was conducted; for data that do not meet the normality assumption, non-parametric tests were employed.</p></section></section><section id="s0055"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results and discussion</h2>
<section id="s0060"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. PCA analysis</h3>
<p id="p0155">The six distinct processing techniques and parameters were shown in <a href="#f0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>A and <a href="#t0005" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>. The PCA model (<a href="#f0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>B) extracted three significant principal components, explaining 45.0% of the total variance in the dataset (R<sup>2</sup>X(cum) = 0.450). Cross-validation confirmed the model's robustness, showing a high predictive ability of 53.1% (Q<sup>2</sup>(cum) = 0.531). The first principal component (PC1) accounted for the majority of the explained variance (27.5%) and also demonstrated the strongest individual predictive power (Q<sup>2</sup> = 0.291). The PCA indicates that the six processing methods can be further categorized into three distinct groups: heat treatment (ZH and FHZH), non-heat treatment (XQ, FH and SG), and shade drying treatment (YG). Notably, although classified within the non-heat-treated category, YG group leads to significant differences characteristic peak when compared to the other non-heat-treated methods.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0005" title="Fig. 1"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/91fc6bf2ab96/gr1.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0005" height="420" width="788" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>PCA score plot of <em>Poria cocos</em> under different processing techniques. A) Six different processing techniques for <em>Poria cocos</em>; B) PCA) score plot derived from the UPLC-MS secondary metabolite profiles of <em>Poria cocos</em> samples subjected to different processing techniques. The 95% confidence ellipse (Hotelling's T<sup>2</sup>) clearly shows distinct clustering according to processing methods. The three-component PCA model demonstrated good separation with moderate explanatory variance (R<sup>2</sup>X(cum) = 0.450) and a predictive ability of Q<sup>2</sup>(cum) = 0.531.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s0065"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Triterpenes database of <em>Poria cocos</em>
</h3>
<p id="p0160">A total of 480 natural products associated with the PubChem taxonomy ID 81056 (<em>Wolfiporia</em> cocos) were retrieved. After removing duplicate compounds based on their compound CIDs, 308 unique compounds remained, as detailed in <strong>Supplementary Materials S3</strong>. Compound classification was performed using the classyfireR tool, with the corresponding code provided in <strong>Supplementary Materials S4</strong> and the classification results presented in Supplementary Materials S5. Additionally, a comprehensive literature review was conducted to incorporate the most recently reported triterpenoid constituents of <em>Poria cocos</em>. Consequently, a dataset comprising 211 triterpenoid compounds was compiled, including information on compound names, molecular formulas, molecular weights, exact masses, InChIKeys, SMILES representations, and molecular structures, as documented in <strong>Supplementary Materials S6</strong>. Compared to previous reviews reported in the literature (<a href="#bb0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0180">Lu et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bb0195" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0195">Nie et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bb0210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0210">Ríos, 2011</a>), this taxonomy-based approach enables rapid batch retrieval of compound information from PubChem for a specific taxonomic group. Notably, the present study expands this number to 211 triterpenoid compounds of <em>Poria cocos</em>, representing a 30.8% increase compared to 159 in the prior review (<a href="#bb0180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0180">Lu et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bb0300" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0300">Xiong et al., 2025</a>).</p></section><section id="s0070"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Molecular network analysis exploration of chemical compounds in <em>Poria cocos</em>
</h3>
<p id="p0165">By uploading the 6 groups of “.mzML” format data to the GNPS platform for molecular network analysis (task analysis result link: <a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=9b599b71250741c08eb1b2b2c2e266ee" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=9b599b71250741c08eb1b2b2c2e266ee</a>). Then, Cytoscape (<a href="#bb0220" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0220">Shannon et al., 2003</a>) tool was applied to draw the network graph, resulting in <a href="#f0010" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>A, where the relative peak intensities of different groups were represented in a pie chart. There are a total of 2959 nodes, with 256 clusters having more than 2 nodes and 1086 clusters having a single node. From the molecular network results of GNPS, it is not optimistic that only a few triterpenoids (represented by red boxes) were found. Through the literature review, no relevant reports were found on predicting the triterpenoid chemical composition in <em>Poria cocos</em> using GNPS. We further conducted GNPS mass spectrometry database statistical analysis, which comprised 14,784 compounds (<a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/result.jsp?task=698fc5a09db74c7492983b3673ff5bf6&amp;view=molecule_explorer_v2_summary&amp;show=true" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/result.jsp?task=698fc5a09db74c7492983b3673ff5bf6&amp;view=molecule_explorer_v2_summary&amp;show=true</a>, exported at Apr. 15, 2025). Following the removal of duplicate SMILES entries, a total of 10,296 compounds remained. Utilizing RDKit (<a href="https://www.rdkit.org/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.rdkit.org/</a>), we converted the SMILES entries to InChIKeys and subsequently classified the chemical substances based on the InChIKeys. It was found that GNPS only contains 120 triterpenoids in 10,296 compounds. Compared with the 211 triterpenoids in <em>Poria cocos</em> we summarized, none of them were included, as documented in <strong>Supplementary Materials S7</strong>. The secondary spectra of each node depicted in <a href="#f0010" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>A were manually analyzed, resulting in the annotation information of the compounds presented in <a href="#f0010" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>B.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0010" title="Fig. 2"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/05bedf1e324c/gr2.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0010" height="407" width="788" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Molecular networking and compound annotation of <em>Poria cocos</em> extracts. A) Molecular network generated in GNPS from the UPLC-MS/MS data of <em>Poria cocos</em> extracts across six processing methods. Nodes represent parent ions, and edges represent spectral similarity, illustrating the relatedness of metabolites; B) A detailed view of an annotated cluster within the network.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s0075"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. MCnebula2 mass spectrometry data calculation annotation and visualization analysis</h3>
<p id="p0170">Considering the aforementioned limitations associated with the use of GNPS, we conducted a comprehensive analysis of the constituents of <em>Poria cocos</em> employing MCnebula2, a mass spectrometry analysis tool developed by our research team in Cao's laboratory. <a href="#f0015" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>A illustrates the parent nebula of compounds in <em>Poria cocos</em>. The top 21 compound classes detected are presented in <a href="#f0015" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>B, categorized across hierarchical levels 2 to 5. Specifically, level 2 (depicted in yellow) includes Organoheterocyclic compounds, Benzenoids; level 3 (red) encompasses Prenol lipids, Benzene and substituted derivatives, Fatty Acyls, and Oxacyclic compounds; level 4 (purple) comprises Ethers, Dicarboxylic acids and derivatives, Carboxylic acid derivatives, Monocarboxylic acids and derivatives, Oxosteroids, “Bile acids, alcohols and derivatives”, Triterpenoids, Steroid esters, Steroid acids; level 5 (blue) consists of Ketones, Primary alcohols, Carboxylic acid esters, Cyclic alcohols and derivatives, Tertiary alcohols, and Polyols. Among these classes, particular attention was directed towards the bioactive triterpenoid compounds (<a href="#bb0090" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0090">Guo et al., 2025</a>) present in <em>Poria cocos</em>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0015" title="Fig. 3"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr3.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/fd006937ba7c/gr3.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0015" height="459" width="765" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0015/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Analyze chemical composition using MCneubla. A) Compounds in <em>Poria cocos</em> parent nebula visualized by MCnebula2; B) Classification of the main chemical components in <em>Poria cocos</em>.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0175"><a href="#f0020" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a> presents a total ion chromatogram (TIC) of <em>Poria cocos</em> obtained under the representative negative ion mode. <a href="#f0025" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a> illustrates the relative peak areas of four standard samples alongside the classification and annotation details of three unique compounds (Compounds 1, 4, and 17) identified within the YG group.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0020" title="Fig. 4"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/eaceb1d87180/gr4.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0020" height="586" width="789" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0020/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>TIC of <em>Poria cocos</em> sample in UPLC-QTOF-MS ESI<sup>−</sup> mode.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0025" title="Fig. 5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr5.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/397722e9f0a1/gr5.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0025" height="389" width="788" alt="Fig. 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0025/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Relative quantitative analysis of chemical composition was conducted using MCneubla.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s0080"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Fragmentation patterns of four standard samples</h3>
<p id="p0180"><a href="#f0030" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a> analyzed the fragmentation behavior of primary and secondary spectra in the negative ion mode for four standard compounds. Peaks 27, 33, 53, and 56 were identified as Poricoic acid B (<em>m/z</em> 483.3111), Poricoic acid A (<em>m/z</em> 497.3270), DehydropachyMic acid (<em>m/z</em> 525.3588), and Dehydrotrametinonic acid (<em>m/z</em> 453.3370), respectively. The fragmentation patterns of these four standards were summarized in <a href="#f0030" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a>. Cleavage predominantly occurs at the -COO or -OH functional groups and methyl substituents on the branched chains, leading to the opening of rings A and B. Additionally, these compounds are susceptible to McLafferty rearrangement and retro-Diels–Alder (RDA) cleavage mechanisms.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0030" title="Fig. 6"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr6.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/447931ee075a/gr6.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0030" height="505" width="788" alt="Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0030/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Fragmentation patterns of four standards in UPLC-QTOF ESI<sup>−</sup> mode.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s0085"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Identification and pharmacological properties of triterpenoids</h3>
<p id="p0185">Prior to executing SIRIUS computational analyses, the SMILES information of triterpenoids contained in <em>Poria cocos</em> was imported into a customized database. By integrating the compounds predicted by SIRIUS with fragment information obtained from secondary mass spectra, in <em>Poria cocos,</em> a total of 56 triterpenoid compounds were identified, and the pharmacological properties were summarized in <a href="#t0010" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>. The findings indicate that <em>Poria cocos</em> exhibits multiple pharmacological properties, including anti-inflammatory, antioxidation, anti-renal fibrosis, antitumour, cytotoxicity to HL60, inhibition of LDH activity, inhibition of TPA-induced EBV-EA, inhibition α-glucosidase activity, MMP-13 inhibitor, moderate cytotoxic activities against MNK-45 cell lines, regulation of glycolipid metabolism, stimulating glucose uptake, improving insulin sensitivity, treating Alzheimer's disease and anti-non-alcoholic fatty liver disease.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/t0010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="p0190">We further compared the unique pharmacological properties of <em>Poria cocos</em> under different processing techniques in <a href="#t0010" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>. We found that only the heat-treated group (FHZH and ZH) did not exhibit the characteristics of MMP3 inhibitors (Compound 16), while all other groups had all the pharmacological properties mentioned above.</p>
<p id="p0195">The chemical structures of the 56 triterpenoid illustrated in <a href="#f0035" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a><strong>,</strong> and six type parent nuclei and their quantities are: type I <strong>(19)</strong> 3,4-Ring opening lanoster-7,9(11)-diene triterpenes, type II <strong>(5)</strong> 3,4-Ring opening lanoster-8-ene triterpenes, type III <strong>(18)</strong> Lanoster-8-ene triterpenes, type IV <strong>(11)</strong> Lanoster-7,9(11)-diene triterpenes, type V <strong>(1)</strong> Cyclodioxy tetracyclic triterpenes, and type V <strong>(1)</strong> Pentacyclic triterpenes, and type VI <strong>(1)</strong> 4, 5-Ring opening triterpenes. The triterpene parent nucleus of <em>Poria cocos</em> is characterized by a relatively stable and rigid framework; however, its notable structural diversity is attributed to extensive substitutions occurring at multiple modification sites. Carboxyl groups are predominantly situated at the C-3 and C-21 positions. Core double bonds are mainly found at the C-7(8) and C-9(11) locations, with less frequent occurrences at the C-5(6), C-6(7), and C-16(17), the latter three arising from the dehydration of hydroxyl groups at C-6 and C-16. Double bonds on the side chain are located at C-24(25). Hydroxyl substitutions exhibit a polymorphic distribution, with primary substitution sites at C-3 and C-16, and secondary sites including C-6, C-15, C-23, C-25, C-26, C-29, and C-30. Importantly, the C-3 position may also be modified by carbonyl or acetyl groups, thereby further enhancing the structural variability of the molecule.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0035" title="Fig. 7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr7.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/81afc1664fa4/gr7.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0035" height="607" width="788" alt="Fig. 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0035/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Chemical structures of 56 triterpenoids annotated in <em>Poria cocos</em>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s0090"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7. Effect of different processing techniques for the parent nucleus and side chains</h3>
<p id="p0200">The non-parametric Kruskal-Wallis test, applied to multiple independent samples, revealed no significant association between the frequency of occurrence of the identical parent nuclei or side chains and the various processing techniques. However, a significant difference was observed between the frequency of occurrence of different parent nuclei and side chains among the 56 triterpenoids. Notably, the type III (Lanoster-8-ene triterpenoid) parent nucleus and the m-type side chain were the most frequently occurring, as illustrated in <a href="#f0040" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>A and B.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0040" title="Fig. 8"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr8.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/21129c9ed83f/gr8.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0040" height="168" width="788" alt="Fig. 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0040/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>A non-parametric Kruskal-Wallis test. A) The frequency of parent nucleus occurrence and processing technology. B) The frequency of side chain occurrence and processing technology. Different letters indicate significant differences between groups (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s0095"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.8. Effect of different processing techniques the triterpenoid types and contents</h3>
<p id="p0205"><a href="#f0045" class="usa-link">Fig. 9</a> and <a href="#t0015" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> present data concerning the compounds identified within six groups. The quantities of identified triterpenoids are as follows: YG (56), ZH (48), FHZH (42), SG (47), and both XQ and FH (43). Notably, 34 triterpenoids are shared across all six groups, while three compounds (Compounds 1, 4, and 17) are exclusive to the YG group. Additionally, Compounds 26, 28, 38, and 45 are unique to the heat-treated groups (FHZH, ZH) and the YG group, whereas Compounds 9, 14, 16, and 21 are specific to the non-heat-treated groups (FH, SG, XQ) and the YG group.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0045" title="Fig. 9"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 9.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/t0015/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn3"><p>Note: The symbol × indicates the absence of peak numbers within the group, whereas the symbol √ denotes their presence.</p></div></div></section><p id="p0210">Using the MetaboAnalyst 6.0 platform (<a href="#bb0200" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0200">Pang et al., 2024</a>), hierarchical clustering analysis was performed on the peak areas of 56 triterpenoids in <em>Poria cocos</em> processed by different techniques. The resulting peak area heatmap (<a href="#f0050" class="usa-link">Fig. 10</a>) displays a color gradient blue (low) to red (high). The analysis revealed distinct clustering patterns: the non-heat-treated samples formed one group, the heat-treated samples formed another, and the YG group clustered separately. These vertical clusters among the different groups correspond well with the PCA results in <a href="#f0005" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>B. It is noteworthy that, although the YG group consisted of samples that did not undergo heat treatment, it activated the dormant <em>Poria cocos</em> sclerotium under optimal conditions, 23 °C and 90% relative humidity, and showed a significantly higher content of triterpenoids compared to other groups, include both the non-heat-treated groups and the heat-treated groups and compounds quantity accounting for 82.1% and 71.4% of triterpenoids, respectively.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0050" title="Fig. 10"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 10.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr10.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/8563fda24afb/gr10.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0050" height="817" width="789" alt="Fig. 10"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0050/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Peak area heat map of 56 triterpenoid chemicals in <em>Poria cocos</em> samples processed by six different techniques. Symbol * indicating significant differences compared to other groups (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0215"><a href="#f0050" class="usa-link">Fig. 10</a>, <a href="#f0055" class="usa-link">Fig. 11</a> illustrate the peak areas of the 56 triterpenoid compounds across the different <em>Poria cocos</em> groups. The YG and ZH groups exhibited a greater number of active components and higher relative intensities of triterpenoid. Notably, Poricoic acid K <strong>(1)</strong>, 25-Hydroxyporicoic acid H <strong>(4)</strong>, and Poricoic acid M <strong>(17)</strong> were exclusively detected in the YG group. Compared to the other five groups, the YG group demonstrated a significant increase in the concentrations of Poricoic acid K <strong>(1)</strong>, Pinicolic acid F <strong>(2)</strong>, Poricoic acid ZD <strong>(3)</strong>, 25-Hydroxyporicoic acid H <strong>(4)</strong>, 3beta,15alpha-Dihydroxy-24-oxolanosta-8-en-21-oic acid <strong>(6)</strong>, 16alpha,25-Dihydroxyeburiconic Acid <strong>(8),</strong> Poricoic acid E <strong>(9)</strong>, Poricoic acid ZI <strong>(16)</strong>, Poricoic acid M <strong>(17)</strong>, Poricoic acid GM <strong>(18)</strong>, Poricoic acid D <strong>(21)</strong>, 26-Hydroxyporicoic acid DM <strong>(22)</strong>, 25-Hydroxy-3-epitumulosic Acid <strong>(23)</strong>, Poricoic acid ZM <strong>(31)</strong>, Poricoic acid A <strong>(33)</strong>, Tumulosic acid <strong>(37)</strong>, Ceanphytamic acid A <strong>(42),</strong> O-Acetylpachymic acid-25-ol <strong>(47)</strong> and Dehydrotrametenolic acid <strong>(56)</strong>; The SG group exhibited a significant elevation in the level of Poricoic acid ZA <strong>(7)</strong> and 3-(2-Hydroxyacetoxy)-5alpha,8alpha-peroxydehydrotumulosic acid <strong>(15)</strong>; Furthermore, the ZH group showed a significant increase in the content of Poricoic acid G <strong>(11),</strong> 3alpha,16alpha,25-Trihydroxylanosta-8,24-dien-21-oic acid <strong>(12)</strong>, Pinicolic acid E <strong>(24),</strong> 16α-Acetoxy-26,27-dimethoxyl-lanosta-8,24(31)-dien-21-oic acid <strong>(25)</strong>, 3,15-<em>O</em>-diacetyl-dehydrotrametenolic acid <strong>(36),</strong> Poricoic acid ZW <strong>(40)</strong>, Dehydroeburicoic acid <strong>(44)</strong> and Eburicoic acid <strong>(51)</strong>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0055" title="Fig. 11"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 11.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr11.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/db6f7eae82c9/gr11.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0055" height="931" width="788" alt="Fig. 11"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0055/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Box plot comparing the relative peak areas of 56 triterpenoid components in six processed products of <em>Poria cocos.</em> Different letters indicate significant differences between groups in the one-way ANOVA and Tukey's multiple comparisons test (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0220">The heat-treated groups demonstrated a higher triterpenoid content relative to the non-heat-treated groups, a phenomenon attributed to the disruption of cell walls at elevated temperatures, which facilitates the release of bioactive components.</p>
<p id="p0225">While both heat treatment and shade drying processing techniques effectively enhance bioactive components, their mechanisms differ fundamentally. Thermal processing drives chemical restructuring through bond cleavage and esterification, whereas the shade drying method achieves biological reactivation via sclerotia germination, preserving native compound integrity while enhancing triterpenoid diversity. This mechanism highlights the distinctive advantage of shade drying processing techniques in mild processing conditions, retaining original bioactive architectures while stimulating metabolic activity, whereas thermal processing sacrifices structural through forced modifications, incurring partial component degradation.</p></section><section id="s0100"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.9. Effect of hyphae germination on triterpenoids</h3>
<p id="p0230">Studying the effect of hyphae germination on triterpenoids aims to increase the content and types of active triterpenoids in <em>Poria cocos</em>, providing a scientific basis for promoting the quality standardization application of <em>Poria cocos</em>. By regulating optimal temperature, relative humidity, and slice thickness of the YG group, we successfully activated the dormant sclerotium of <em>Poria cocos</em> (<a href="#f0060" class="usa-link">Fig. 12</a>A). The YG group fungal mycelium was cultured on potato dextrose agar (PDA) medium (<a href="#f0060" class="usa-link">Fig. 12</a>B and C). Under an optical microscope, the mycelium is tubular, with distinct septa and numerous branches; the spores are elliptical (<a href="#f0060" class="usa-link">Fig. 12</a>D). The mycelium was taxonomically confirmed as <em>Poria cocos</em> using fungal ITS rRNA gene amplicon sequencing. The phylogenetic tree clearly demonstrated that our strain clustered within the <em>Wolfiporia</em> cocos (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/KX421293.1" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">KX421293.1</a>) clade with a high bootstrap support of 99% (<a href="#f0060" class="usa-link">Fig. 12</a>E). Under these controlled conditions, the dormant sclerotium of <em>Poria cocos</em> resumed growth and expansion, producing new mycelia and transitioning into a germination-like state, characterized by an increased concentration of triterpenoid bioactive compounds. Consequently, we propose that the distinctive chemical profile of YG group <em>Poria cocos</em> primarily results from the shade drying treatment.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f0060" title="Fig. 12"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 12.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12906123_gr12.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e9c6/12906123/69eccdf455d1/gr12.jpg" loading="lazy" id="lk0060" height="427" width="788" alt="Fig. 12"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f0060/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Morphological, molecular identification, and triterpenoid relative content Spearman correlation of culturable fungal mycelium from <em>Poria cocos</em> (YG group). A) Representative sample of the YG group of <em>Poria cocos</em>; B—C) Morphology of fungal isolates cultured on PDA medium after 7 days, showing the colony surface (B) and reverse side (C); D) Photomicrographs of the mycelial structure observed under an optical microscope at 40×; E) Neighbor-Joining phylogenetic tree based on ITS sequences, showing the relationship of the isolated strain with closely related species. Bootstrap support values (%) from 1000 replicates are shown at the nodes; F) Spearman rank correlation analysis between the average peak areas of triterpene compounds in the FLS and YG groups. The analysis revealed a significant positive correlation (<em>r</em><sub><em>s</em></sub> = 0.660, <em>n</em> = 20, <em>p</em> = 0.002).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0235">The TIC of <em>Poria cocos</em> mycelia (FLS) and the YG group are presented in the <strong>Supplementary Materials S8</strong>. The LC-MS analysis revealed that the FLS group contained 20 triterpenoid compounds that were significantly increased (<em>p</em> &lt; 0.05) compared to the YG group, including Compounds 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 16, 17, 18, 21, 22, 23, 31, 33, 37, 42, 47, and 56. A Spearman's correlation (<a href="#bb0330" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0330">Zar, 2005</a>) was run to determine the relationship between the triterpenes' peak area of the YG and the FLS. There was a strong, positive monotonic correlation between the accumulation of 20 triterpenes in the <em>Poria cocos</em> YG and FLS groups (<em>r</em><sub><em>s</em></sub> = 0.660, <em>n</em> = 20, <em>p</em> = 0.002, <a href="#f0060" class="usa-link">Fig. 12</a>G). The previous study (<a href="#bb0335" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bb0335">Zeng et al., 2024</a>) had identified hydroxyl and carboxyl functional groups as key contributors to the bioactivity of <em>Poria cocos</em>. The mycelia demonstrated increased hydroxylation and a greater proportion of open-ring triterpenoids compared to other processing methods, indicating that these structural modifications may contribute to the improved bioactivity of <em>Poria cocos</em>. All detected compounds in the FLS group were categorized into Type I, II, III, and IV triterpenes (<a href="#f0035" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>). Specifically, Type I comprised nine compounds characterized as 3,4-ring-opened lanoster-7,9(11)-diene triterpenes, modifications primarily localized at the R2 position. Type II included three compounds identified as 3,4-ring-opened lanoster-8-ene triterpenes, with predominantly modified at the R4 position, exhibiting a boat-chair conformation of the ABCD tetracyclic steroidal framework. Type III consisted of seven compounds conforming to lanoster-8-ene triterpenes, with modifications mainly at the R3 position. Type IV consisted of one compound conforming to lanoster-7,9(11)-diene triterpenes, with modifications mainly at the R4 position.</p></section></section><section id="s0105"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Conclusions</h2>
<p id="p0240">In this study, we built a database containing 211 triterpenoids from <em>Poria cocos</em>, and a total of 56 differentially expressed triterpenoids were identified. Among them, there are significant differences in the frequency of types of parent nuclei and side chains. Parent nuclei Lanoster-8-ene triterpenoid and m-type side chains have the highest frequency of occurrence. Subsequently, we evaluate the effect of six distinct processing techniques on triterpenoids. The shade drying process demonstrated an increased concentration of triterpenoids and richer components compared to other treatments. In order to explain these changes, we verified the previous experimental results, using an optical microscope and the rDNA ITS region sequences molecular marker technique on the mycelium of the YG group, and identified it as still the species of <em>Poria cocos</em> (Schw.) Wolf. Further, we conducted Spearman correlation analysis on the significantly increased triterpenoids in the YG and FLS groups of <em>Poria cocos</em>. The accumulation of triterpenoids in the YG group showed a strong positive monotonic correlation (<em>r</em><sub><em>s</em></sub> = 0.660, <em>p</em> = 0.002) with the FLS group, which was attributed to the temperature (23 °C) and humidity (RH 90%)-controlled shade drying treatment used in the shade drying process, effectively activating dormant fungi in <em>Poria cocos</em>.</p>
<p id="p0245">In summary, the shade drying processing technique of <em>Poria cocos</em> demonstrated excellent performance and deserves further research. This is the first study that SIRIUS-MCnuble has been used to analyze <em>Poria cocos</em>, and the analysis method has good potential, which is worthy of application, emphasizing the considerable potential of AI in deciphering complex food research.</p></section><section id="s0115"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">CRediT authorship contribution statement</h2>
